have enough basic sections, we may form the Courant algebroid relations
Q(K)
and
Q(K_{1})
, which fit into the diagram

(3.24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}{}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(K_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(K)}

We consider the composition of
Q(K)
with
Q(K_{1})^{\top}
, and check the conditions in Propositions
3.19
and
3.20
.

It can be shown that the submanifolds
\text{gr}(\varpi_{1})^{\top}\times\text{gr}(\varpi)
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times\Delta(M)\times{\mathcal{Q}}
intersect cleanly, and the projection
\text{gr}(\varpi)\diamond\text{gr}(\varpi_{1})^{\top}\to\text{gr}(\pi)
is a smooth surjective submersion. For
(q_{1},\pi(q_{1}))\in\text{gr}(\pi)
, the space

(3.25)

\displaystyle\big(Q(K)\diamond Q(K_{1})^{\top}\big)_{(q_{1},\pi(q_{1}))}=\set{\big(\natural_{E_{1}}(e),e,e,\natural(e)\big)\ \big.}{\ e\in K_{m}^{\perp}}

is isomorphic to
K^{\perp}_{m}
, for any
m\in M
such that
q_{1}=\varpi_{1}(m)
, and hence has constant dimension. Finally, the projection
Q(K)\diamond Q(K_{1})^{\top}\to Q(K)\circ Q(K_{1})^{\top}
has kernel given by
K\cap K_{1}=K_{1}
, and hence has constant rank.

Thus the conditions
7
of both Propositions
3.19
and
3.20
are satisfied, and the composition is clean, giving the relation

(3.26)

\displaystyle R({\mathcal{F}}_{1})\coloneqq Q(K)\circ Q(K_{1})^{\top}=\set{(\natural_{E_{1}}(e),\natural(e))\ \big.}{\ e\in K^{\perp}}\ \subset\ \underline{E}{}_{1}\times\overline{\underline{E}}\ ,

supported on the graph
\text{gr}(\pi)
.

Example
3.22
may be seen as the foliated reduction counterpart of the well-known case of reduction of a
{\mathsf{G}}
-equivariant exact Courant algebroid by a closed Lie subgroup
8
8

8

This is usually chosen to be Lagrangian with respect to the split-signature pairing.

{\mathsf{H}}\subset{\mathsf{G}}
that represents one of the building blocks of Poisson-Lie T-duality (see e.g.
[
12
, Section 5.1]
). We will explore this case in detail along the lines of
[
12
,
13
]
in future work, including how to properly deal with reduction of generalised metrics.

4.
Courant Algebroid Relations as Isometries

In this section we review important ideas of
[
13
, Section 5]
. We then generalise these to cases of interest to us, proceeding to define generalised isometries for transverse generalised metrics.

4.1.
Generalised Metrics

We wish to know when a given Courant algebroid relation is able to carry geometric structure between Courant algebroids.
In particular, we are interested in relations between generalised metrics as a building block for our approach to T-duality.

Definition 4.1
.

A
generalised metric
on a Courant algebroid
E
is an automorphism
\tau\colon E\to E
covering the identity with
\tau^{2}=\mathds{1}_{E}
which, together with the pairing
\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle
, defines a positive-definite fibrewise metric

(4.2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}(e,e^{\prime})=\left\langle e,\tau(e^{\prime})\right\rangle

on
E
, for
e,e^{\prime}\in E
. We denote the Courant algebroid
E
endowed with a generalised metric
\tau
by
(E,\tau)
.

Generalised metrics on exact Courant algebroids are characterised as follows, see e.g.
[
6
, Proposition 3.5]
for details.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
E
be an exact Courant algebroid over
M
. A generalised metric
{\mathcal{G}}
, as in Equation (
4.2
), uniquely determines a pair
(g,b)\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(\text{\Large$\odot$}^{2}T^{*}M)\times\mathsf{\Omega}^{2}(M)
where
g
is non-degenerate. Conversely, a Riemannian metric
g
and a two-form
b
on
M
define a generalised metric
{\mathcal{G}}
given by

(4.4)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}=\begin{pmatrix}g-b\,g^{-1}\,b&b\,g^{-1}\\
-g^{-1}\,b&g^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

on the isomorphic twisted standard Courant algebroid
\mathbb{T}M=TM\oplus T^{*}M.

An alternative characterisation of generalised metrics is

Definition 4.5
.

A
generalised metric
is a subbundle
V^{+}\subseteq E
which is a maximal positive-definite subbundle of
E
with respect to
\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle
. We denote the Courant algebroid
E
endowed with a generalised metric
V^{+}
by
(E,V^{+})
.

Remark 4.6

(
Generalised Metrics as Subbundles
)
.

Definitions
4.1
and
4.5
are equivalent, see e.g.
[
6
, Proposition 3.3]
: the maximal positive-definite subbundle
V^{+}
is the
+1
-eigenbundle for the generalised metric
\tau
. Indeed, if
\tau
is given by
g
and
b
as in Proposition
4.3
, then one can show

(4.7)

\displaystyle V^{+}=\text{gr}(g+b)=\set{v+\iota_{v}(g+b)\in\mathbb{T}M\ |\ v\in TM}\ .

The
-1
-eigenbundle of
\tau
is given by
V^{-}=\text{gr}(-g+b)=(V^{+})^{\perp}
.

Since Definitions
4.1
and
4.5
are equivalent, we can switch between the notations
(E,\tau)
and
(E,V^{+})
.

4.2.
Generalised Isometries

We now have enough machinery to talk about a notion of isometries of generalised metrics for Courant algebroid relations, following
[
13
]
.

Definition 4.8
.

Suppose
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
is a Courant algebroid relation supported on a submanifold
C\subseteq M_{1}\times M_{2}
. Let
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
be generalised metrics for
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
respectively, and set
\tau\coloneqq\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}
. Then
R
is a
generalised isometry between
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
if
\tau(R)=R
.

It follows from the definitions that
\tau=\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}
is a generalised metric for
E_{1}\times E_{2}
. It is not, however, a generalised metric for
E_{1}\times\overline{E}_{2}
: the
\pm 1
-eigenbundles are not necessarily maximally positive-definite with respect to the pairing on
E_{1}\times\overline{E}_{2}
. See
[
13
, Remark 5.2]
for details.

Remark 4.9

(
Classical Generalised Isometries
)
.

If
R
is a classical Courant algebroid morphism, then
R=\text{gr}(\Phi)
for some vector bundle map
\Phi:E_{1}\to E_{2}
covering a smooth map
\varphi\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
. For
\text{gr}(\Phi)
to be a generalised isometry, the equation

(4.10)

\displaystyle\tau_{2}\circ\Phi=\Phi\circ\tau_{1}

must hold, or equivalently

(4.11)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{2}\big(\Phi(e_{1}),\Phi(e_{1}^{\prime})\big)_{\varphi(m_{1})}={\mathcal{G}}_{1}(e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime})_{m_{1}}\ ,

for each
e_{1},e_{1}^{\prime}\in(E_{1})_{m_{1}}
with
m_{1}\in M_{1}
, for the metrics
{\mathcal{G}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
induced by
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
respectively. This justifies the terminology ‘generalised isometry’
[
13
]
.

For later purposes, let us stress that a further equivalent condition for a classical Courant algebroid morphism
\Phi
to be a generalised isometry is

(4.12)

\displaystyle\Phi(V_{1}^{+})=V_{2}^{+}\ ,

where
V_{i}^{+}=\ker(\mathds{1}_{E_{i}}-\tau_{i})
is the
+1
-eigenbundle of
\tau_{i}
for
i=1,2.

When
\Phi
is a Courant algebroid isomorphism, generalised isometries are characterised by

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be exact Courant algebroids over
M_{1}
and
M_{2},
respectively, and suppose they are endowed with generalised metrics
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
, respectively. A Courant algebroid isomorphism
\Phi\colon E_{1}\to E_{2},
covering
\varphi\in\mathsf{Diff}(M_{1},M_{2}),
is a generalised isometry if and only if

(4.14)

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}g_{2}=g_{1}\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad\varphi^{*}b_{2}=b_{1}+B\ ,

where, by using the canonical isomorphism
E_{i}\simeq TM_{i}\oplus TM^{*}_{i}

given by any isotropic splitting,
\tau_{i}
is determined by the pair
(g_{i},b_{i})
for
i=1,2,
and (with a slight abuse of notation)
\Phi=\overline{\varphi}\circ{\,\rm e}\,^{B}\,
for some
B\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{2}(M_{1}).

Proof.

By choosing a splitting for
E_{1},
its generalised metric is
V_{1}^{+}\simeq\mathrm{gr}(g_{1}+b_{1}).
It then follows that

(4.15)

\displaystyle\Phi\big(v_{1}+\iota_{v_{1}}(g_{1}+b_{1})\big)=\varphi_{*}(v_{1})+(\varphi^{-1})^{*}\big(\iota_{v_{1}}(g_{1}+b_{1}+B)\big)\ ,

for all
v_{1}\in TM_{1},
is an element in
V_{2}^{+}
if and only if
\varphi^{*}g_{2}=g_{1}
and
\varphi^{*}b_{2}=b_{1}+B
, since
V_{2}^{+}\simeq\mathrm{gr}(g_{2}+b_{2})
in the chosen splitting for
E_{2}
.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

Proposition
4.13
reduces to
[
14
, Proposition 2.41]
when
M_{1}=M_{2}=M
and
\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}=\tau,
i.e. in this case
\Phi
is a generalised isometry if and only if
\varphi^{*}g=g
and
B=0.

Following the equivalence between Definitions
4.1
and
4.5
, we can formulate an alternative definition of generalised isometry in terms of the eigenbundles of the automorphisms
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}.

Definition 4.17
.

Suppose
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
is a Courant algebroid relation supported on a submanifold
C\subseteq M_{1}\times M_{2}
. Let
V_{1}^{+}
and
V_{2}^{+}
be generalised metrics for
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
respectively, and set
V_{i}^{-}\coloneqq(V_{i}^{+})^{\perp}
. Let
\cal V^{\pm}=V_{1}^{\pm}\times V_{2}^{\pm}
. Then
R
is a
generalised isometry between
V_{1}^{+}
and
V_{2}^{+}
if

(4.18)

\displaystyle R_{c}=(\cal V_{c}^{+}\cap R_{c})\oplus(\cal V_{c}^{-}\cap R_{c})\ ,

for each
c\in C
.

We briefly comment on the pointwise nature of this definition, which will be a theme throughout this section. Due to the nature of relations, we only consider their pointwise description, and do not demand that our definition holds globally over the submanifold
C
that
R
is supported on. In particular, in Equation (
4.18
) we do not demand that
\cal V^{\pm}\big|_{C}\cap R
are both subbundles supported on
C,

since the intersection might not have constant rank.
We could distinguish from cases where global descriptions (on
C
) are possible, however this is not necessary for the description of generalised isometries, as made clear through

Proposition 4.19
.

Definition
4.8
and Definition
4.17
are equivalent.

Proof.

Suppose
\tau(R)=R
. The bundles
\cal V^{\pm}
are the
\pm 1
-eigenbundles for
\tau=\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}
. Take
c\in C
and let
r\in R_{c}
. Since
E_{1}\times\overline{E}_{2}=\cal V^{+}\oplus\cal V^{-}
, we can write
r=r^{+}+r^{-}
, for
r^{\pm}\in\cal V^{\pm}_{c}
. Thus

(4.20)

\displaystyle\tau(r^{+}+r^{-})=r^{+}-r^{-}\ \in\ R_{c}\ .

Hence both
r^{+}+r^{-}
and
r^{+}-r^{-}
are in
R_{c}
, so that
r^{+},r^{-}\in R_{c}
, and it follows that
R_{c}\subseteq(\cal V_{c}^{+}\cap R_{c})\oplus(\cal V_{c}^{-}\cap R_{c})
, hence Equation (
4.18
) holds.

Conversely, if
R_{c}
decomposes as in Equation (
4.18
) for every
c\in C
, then each
r\in R_{c}
can be written as
r=r^{+}+r^{-}
where
r^{\pm}\in\cal V^{\pm}_{c}\cap R_{c}
. Hence
\tau(r)=r^{+}-r^{-}\in R_{c}
, so
\tau(R_{c})=R_{c}
. Since this holds for every
c\in C
, it follows that
\tau(R)=R
.
∎

Composition of generalised isometries works well: the composition
R^{\prime}\circ R
of two Courant algebroid relations that are generalised isometries and that compose cleanly is also a generalised isometry, see
[
13
, Proposition 5.7]
.

Example 4.21

(
\boldsymbol{Q(K)}
is not a Generalised Isometry
)
.

Let us look at the relation
Q(K)
of Section
3.2
, where
K
is an isotropic subbundle
9
9

9

We always assume that
K
has non-zero rank.
inducing a regular foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
of
M
given by the integral manifolds of
\rho(K)
with smooth leaf space
{\mathcal{Q}}=M/{\mathcal{F}}
and projection map
\varpi:M\to{\mathcal{Q}}
. Take generalised metrics
\mathscr{V}^{+}\subset E
and
V^{+}\subset\underline{E}
: a generalised metric
V^{+}
may be constructed from
\mathscr{V}^{+}
whenever
W=\mathscr{V}^{+}
satisfies Equation (
2.45
) of Proposition
2.44
. This is similar to
[
13
, Example 5.6]
, where Equation (
2.45
) is tantamount to
{\mathsf{G}}
-equivariance; therein Vysoký concludes that
Q(K)
is not a generalised isometry.

In our case we argue as follows.
If
Q(K)
is a generalised isometry between
\mathscr{V}^{+}
and
V^{+}
, at a point
(m,\varpi(m))\in\text{gr}(\varpi)
we could write

(4.22)

\displaystyle Q(K)_{(m,\varpi(m))}=\big({\mathcal{V}}_{(m,\varpi(m))}^{+}\cap Q(K)_{(m,\varpi(m))}\big)\oplus\big({\mathcal{V}}_{(m,\varpi(m))}^{-}\cap Q(K)_{(m,\varpi(m))}\big)\ ,

where
{\mathcal{V}}^{\pm}=\mathscr{V}^{\pm}\times V^{\pm}.

Since
\big(k,0^{\underline{E}}_{\varpi(m)}\big)\in Q(K)_{(m,\varpi(m))}
, for
k\in K_{m}
, this implies

(4.23)

\displaystyle\big(k,0^{\underline{E}}_{\varpi(m)}\big)=\big(k^{+},0^{\underline{E}}_{\varpi(m)}\big)+\big(k^{-},0^{\underline{E}}_{\varpi(m)}\big)\ ,

where
\big(k^{\pm},0^{\underline{E}}_{\varpi(m)}\big)\in{\mathcal{V}}_{(m,\varpi(m))}^{\pm}\cap Q(K)_{(m,\varpi(m))}
. Since these live inside
Q(K)
, and the kernel of
\natural
is
K
, it follows that
k^{\pm}\in K_{m}
.
Thus
k^{\pm}\in K_{m}\cap\mathscr{V}_{m}^{\pm}
. In particular

(4.24)

\displaystyle 0<\left\langle k^{+},k^{+}\right\rangle_{E}=0\ ,

which is a contradiction. Hence
Q(K)
cannot be a generalised isometry.

Notice that the isotropy of
K
here plays a crucial role. It is shown in
[
13
, Example 5.6]
that
Q(K)
may be a generalised isometry for non-isotropic
{\mathsf{G}}
-actions only if
K\cap K^{\perp}=\set{0}.
We will explore non-isotropic reductions by foliations in future work.

Example
4.21
motivates a characterising property of generalised isometries given by

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
be a generalised isometry between
\tau_{1}\in\mathsf{Aut}(E_{1})
and
\tau_{2}\in\mathsf{Aut}(E_{2})
supported on
C\subseteq M_{1}\times M_{2}
. For each
c=(m_{1},m_{2})\in C
, define subsets
K_{1}\subset E_{1}
and
K_{2}\subset E_{2}
by

(4.26)

\displaystyle(K_{1})_{m_{1}}\times\set{0^{E_{2}}_{m_{2}}}=R_{c}\cap\big((E_{1})_{m_{1}}\times\set{0_{m_{2}}^{E_{2}}}\big)\ ,

(4.27)

\displaystyle\set{0^{E_{1}}_{m_{1}}}\times(K_{2})_{m_{2}}=R_{c}\cap\big(\set{0^{E_{1}}_{m_{1}}}\times(E_{2})_{m_{2}}\big)\ .

Then
(K_{i})_{m_{i}}=\set{0^{E_{i}}_{m_{i}}}
for
i=1,2
.

Proof.

Let
c=(m_{1},m_{2})\in C
and
k_{1}\in(K_{1})_{m_{1}}
. Then

\displaystyle(\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2})(k_{1},0)=\big(\tau_{1}(k_{1}),0\big)\ \in\ (K_{1})_{m_{1}}\times\set{0^{E_{2}}_{m_{2}}}\ ,

hence
(K_{1})_{m_{1}}
is invariant under
\tau_{1}
. Since
R
is isotropic, it follows that
(K_{1})_{m_{1}}
is also isotropic, as
0=\left\langle(k_{1},0),(k^{\prime}_{1},0)\right\rangle=\left\langle k_{1},k^{\prime}_{1}\right\rangle_{E_{1}}-\left\langle 0,0\right\rangle_{E_{2}}
for all
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime}\in(K_{1})_{m_{1}}
, hence

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{1}(k_{1},k_{1})=\left\langle k_{1},\tau_{1}(k_{1})\right\rangle_{E_{1}}=0\ .

Therefore
(K_{1})_{m_{1}}=\set{0^{E_{1}}_{m_{1}}}
for every
m_{1}\in{\rm pr}_{1}(C)
.

Similarly
(K_{2})_{m_{2}}=\set{0^{E_{2}}_{m_{2}}}
for every
m_{2}\in{\rm pr}_{2}(C)
.
∎

A useful uniqueness result for generalised metrics now follows from

Corollary 4.28
.

Let
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
be a generalised isometry between
\tau_{1}\in\mathsf{Aut}(E_{1})
and
\tau_{2}\in\mathsf{Aut}(E_{2})
which is also a generalised isometry between
\tau_{1}\in\mathsf{Aut}(E_{1})
and
\tau_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Aut}(E_{2})
. Then
\tau_{2}|_{{\rm pr}_{2}(R)}=\tau_{2}^{\prime}|_{{\rm pr}_{2}(R)}
, where
{\rm pr}_{2}:E_{1}\times\overline{E}_{2}\to\overline{E}_{2}
is the projection.

Proof.

Let
(e_{1},e_{2})\in R
. Then
(\tau_{1}(e_{1}),\tau_{2}(e_{2}))\in R
and
(\tau_{1}(e_{1}),\tau_{2}^{\prime}(e_{2}))\in R
, therefore
(0,\tau_{2}(e_{2})-\tau_{2}^{\prime}(e_{2}))\in R
. Hence
\tau_{2}(e_{2})=\tau_{2}^{\prime}(e_{2})
for all
e_{2}\in{\rm pr}_{2}(R)
by Proposition
4.25
.
∎

4.3.
Transverse Generalised Isometries

Example
4.21
and Proposition
4.25
highlight the need to go beyond the notion of generalised isometry.
In particular, we wish to extend the definition of generalised isometry to the case when the subsets
K_{i}\subset E_{i}
defined by Equations (
4.26
) and (
4.27
) are non-trivial. For this, we assume from now on that the subsets
K_{i}
are subbundles supported on
M_{i}.
Hence
{\rm pr}_{i}(C)=M_{i}
, where
{\rm pr}_{i}\colon M_{1}\times M_{2}\to M_{i}
are the Cartesian projections for
i=1,2
.

4.3.1.
Transverse Generalised Metrics

We recall the notion of transverse generalised metrics, as first introduced in
[
34
]
, which will aid in understanding the properties of our extension of generalised isometry.

Definition 4.29
.

Let
E
be an exact Courant algebroid over
M
and
K
an involutive isotropic subbundle of
E
. A subbundle
W\subset E
of rank
\mathrm{rk}(W)=\dim(M)
is a
pre-
K
-
transverse generalised metric
if

K\subset W\subset K^{\perp}
and

(4.30)

\displaystyle\langle w,w\rangle_{E}>0\ ,

for all
w\in W
with
w\notin K
. A pre-
K
-transverse generalised metric
W
is a
K
-
transverse generalised metric
if it is invariant with respect to
K
, in the sense that

(4.31)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K),\mathsf{\Gamma}(W)\rrbracket_{E}\subseteq\mathsf{\Gamma}(W)\ .

An exact Courant algebroid
E
endowed with a transverse generalised metric
W
is denoted by
(E,W)
.

Note that this is the same condition appearing in the context of reducible subbundles of exact Courant algebroids, see Remark
2.47
.

Remark 4.32

(
Transverse Generalised Metrics as Graphs
)
.

Similarly to Remark
4.6
(see Equation (
4.7
)), suppose that the restriction of the anchor
\rho_{E}|_{K}\colon K\to TM
to
K
is injective, and that
W
is a pre-
K
-transverse generalised metric. Then in a given splitting, there is a (degenerate) symmetric bilinear pairing
g
and a (degenerate) two-form
b
, with
\ker(b)\supset\ker(g)
, such that

(4.33)

\displaystyle W\simeq\mathrm{gr}(g+b)=\set{v+\iota_{v}(g+b)\in\mathbb{T}M\ |\ v\in TM}\ .

In this splitting, we have the isomorphism
K\simeq\ker(g)
.

If
W
is a
K
-transverse generalised metric, then

(4.34)

\displaystyle\pounds_{X}g=\pounds_{X}b=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\iota_{X}H=0\ ,

for every
X\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(\rho_{E}(K))
, where
H\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{3}_{\rm cl}(M)
represents the Ševera class of
E
. The converse is also true.

The result of Remark
4.32
allows an interpretation in terms of Riemannian submersions and Riemannian foliations, as shown in
[
34
]
.

Example 4.35

(
Riemannian Foliations
)
.

This construction extends to any manifold
(M,g)
endowed with a foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
such that the degenerate symmetric bilinear tensor
g
satisfies
\ker(g)=T{\mathcal{F}}
and is leaf-invariant:

(4.36)

\pounds_{X}g=0\ ,

for all
X\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(T{\mathcal{F}}).
In other words,
(M,g,{\mathcal{F}})
is a Riemannian foliation. It is easy to see that

(4.37)

W=\mathrm{gr}(g)

is a
K
-transverse generalised metric, where
K\simeq T{\mathcal{F}}
is an involutive isotropic subbundle of
\mathbb{T}M
and

K^{\perp}=TM\oplus\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}).

4.3.2.
Relations for Transverse Generalised Metrics

The main goal of this section may be rephrased as giving a definition of isometry of transverse generalised metrics. In order to provide more insight into what our definition should look like, let us first explore the properties of the most natural way to extend generalised isometries: by adapting Equation (
4.12
) to (pre-)transverse generalised metrics.

Remark 4.38

(
Isomorphisms and Transverse Generalised Metrics
)
.

Suppose
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are exact Courant algebroids over
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, respectively, which are isomorphic via a map
\Phi\colon E_{1}\to E_{2}
covering a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
.
Suppose
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are involutive isotropic subbundles of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, respectively, and that
W_{i}
are pre-
K_{i}
-transverse generalised metrics for
i=1,2
.
The natural definition would be to call
\Phi
an isometry of these metrics if
\Phi(W_{1})=W_{2}
. This turns out to be too restrictive.

Let us discuss the conditions arising from this choice. Upon choosing splittings for
E_{i},
we have
\Phi=\overline{\varphi}\circ{\,\rm e}\,^{B}\,
for some
B\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{2}(M_{1})
, and
W_{i}\simeq\mathrm{gr}(g_{i}+b_{i})
for some
g_{i}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(\text{\Large$\odot$}^{2}T^{*}M_{i})
and
b_{i}\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{2}(M_{i}).
As in Proposition
4.13
,
\Phi(W_{1})=W_{2}
if and only if
\varphi^{*}g_{2}=g_{1}
and
\varphi^{*}b_{2}=b_{1}+B.

However, the condition that
\varphi
should map the degenerate symmetric tensors
g_{i}
into each other implies that

(4.39)

\displaystyle\varphi_{*}\big(\ker(g_{1})\big)=\ker(g_{2})\ ,

which is equivalent to requiring
\Phi(K_{1})=K_{2}.
In particular, if
M_{i}
are endowed with Riemannian foliations, by Example
4.35
this is tantamount to requiring that
\varphi
is a foliation-preserving diffeomorphism.

We would like to introduce a notion of generalised isometry for transverse generalised metrics that is not constrained to being foliation-preserving. This choice is motivated by the possibility to formalise the description of T-duality where T-dual manifolds may not be diffeomorphic, and hence the foliations defining the reductions to these backgrounds cannot be bijectively mapped into each other (see Section
5
for more details).
Instead we consider
W^{+}_{i}\coloneqq W_{i}/K_{i}
. We then require
\Phi(\widetilde{W}^{+}_{1})=\widetilde{W}^{+}_{2}
for some lifts
\widetilde{W}^{+}_{i}
of
W^{+}_{i}
to
E_{i}
. In order to extend this condition to a general Courant algebroid relation, we mimic Definition
4.17
using this idea.

Definition 4.40
.

Let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be Courant algebroids over
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, respectively, and let
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
be a Courant algebroid relation supported on
C\subset M_{1}\times M_{2}
.
Suppose
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are involutive isotropic subbundles of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, respectively, and that
W_{i}
are pre-
K_{i}
-transverse generalised metrics for
i=1,2
. Set
W^{+}_{i}\coloneqq W_{i}/K_{i}
and
W^{-}_{i}\coloneqq W_{i}^{\perp}/K_{i}
.

Then
R
is a
transverse generalised isometry
10
10

10

We are aware that our terminology ‘transverse generalised isometry’ might be a misnomer, since these relations do not necessarily preserve the subbundles
K_{i}
defining the transversality conditions. We chose this name for the sake of simplicity, since the
K_{i}
-preserving case is included in our definition.

between
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
if there are pointwise lifts
\widetilde{W}^{\pm}_{i}\subseteq W_{i}
of
W^{\pm}_{i}
to
E_{i}
such that

(4.41)

\displaystyle R_{c}\cap(\cal W^{+}_{c}\oplus\cal W^{-}_{c})=(R_{c}\cap\cal W^{+}_{c})\oplus(R_{c}\cap\cal W^{-}_{c})\ ,

for all
c\in C
, where
\cal W^{\pm}=\widetilde{W}^{\pm}_{1}\times\widetilde{W}^{\pm}_{2}
. A transverse generalised isometry
R
is
regular
if the lifts
\widetilde{W}_{i}^{\pm}
are subbundles of
E_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

Note that the inclusion
\supseteq
in Equation (
4.41
) always holds.

Definition
4.40
may be better understood by looking at the case of a classical Courant algebroid morphism.

Remark 4.42

(
Lifts for Transverse Generalised Isometries
)
.

The lift
\widetilde{W}^{+}_{i}
is a choice of subbundle of
W_{i}
such that

(4.43)

\displaystyle\left\langle w_{i},w_{i}\right\rangle_{E_{i}}>0\ ,

for every
w_{i}\in\widetilde{W}^{+}_{i}
. In other words, it is a choice of splitting
s^{+}_{m_{i}}\colon(W^{+}_{i})_{m_{i}}\to(W_{i})_{m_{i}}
, at each point
m_{i}\in M_{i}
, of the short exact sequence

(4.44)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow(K_{i})_{m_{i}}\longrightarrow(W_{i})_{m_{i}}\longrightarrow(W^{+}_{i})_{m_{i}}\longrightarrow 0\ ,

with
(\widetilde{W}^{+}_{i})_{m_{i}}=s^{+}_{m_{i}}\big((W_{i}^{+})_{m_{i}}\big)
. Similarly, the lift
\widetilde{W}^{-}_{i}
is a choice of negative definite subbundle with respect to the pairing
\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle_{E_{i}}
.

We may think of
\widetilde{W}_{i}^{+}
as the counterpart of
V_{i}^{+}
for transverse generalised metrics, whereas
K
can be interpreted as the degenerate part of
W_{i}
. Definition
4.40
is thus the analogue of Definition
4.17
such that only the positive definite components of
W_{i}
are related.
Note that we recover Definition
4.17
for generalised metrics when
K_{i}
is the zero subbundle.

If the splitting
s^{+}
varies smoothly, then we may consider the short exact sequence (
4.44
) to be a short exact sequence of vector bundles and the splitting is a bundle map, thus
R
is a regular transverse generalised isometry. This will be the case in Section
5
.

The main case in which regular transverse generalised isometries exist is given by

Theorem 4.45
.

Consider the reduction relation
Q(K)\colon E\rightarrowtail\underline{E}
over
\varpi\colon M\to\cal Q
of Section
3.2
and let
V^{+}\subset{\underline{E}}
be a generalised metric on
\underline{E}.
There exists a unique
K
-transverse generalised metric
W
on
E
such that
Q(K)
is a regular transverse generalised isometry between
W
and
V^{+}
.

Conversely, if there is a
K
-transverse generalised metric
W
on
E
, then
V^{+}\coloneqq\natural(W)
is a generalised metric on
\underline{E}
and
Q(K)
is a regular transverse generalised isometry between
W
and
V^{+}
.

Proof.

Take a generalised metric
V^{+}
on
\underline{E}
. Recall that for every
q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
there is an isomorphism
\mathscr{J}_{q,m}\colon\underline{E}{}_{q}\to K^{\perp}_{m}/K_{m}
given by Equation (
2.38
). Define
W_{m}^{+}=\mathscr{J}_{q,m}(V_{q}^{+})
. For two points
m,m^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{q}
, take
w^{+}\in W^{+}_{m}
and
w^{\prime+}\in W^{+}_{m^{\prime}}
such that
\mathscr{J}^{-1}_{q,m}(w^{+})=\mathscr{J}^{-1}_{q,m^{\prime}}(w^{\prime+})
. By the construction in the sketched proof of Theorem
2.34
, there is a basic section
w
such that, under the projection
K^{\perp}\to K^{\perp}/K
,
w
maps to
w^{+}
at
m
and
w^{\prime+}
at
m^{\prime}
. Take
W
to be the span of such basic sections
w
.
Then
K\subset W\subset K^{\perp}
and
\left\langle w,w\right\rangle_{E}>0
for every
w\in W
such that
w\notin K
. Hence
W
is a pre-
K
-transverse generalised metric on
E
.

By Remark
2.47
, for
w\in W
we can write
w=\sum_{i}\,f_{i}\,w_{i}
, for
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}(M)
(so that the sum is locally finite) and
w_{i}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\rm{bas}}(W)
. Then

(4.46)

\displaystyle\llbracket k,w\rrbracket_{E}=\sum_{i}\,f_{i}\,\llbracket k,w_{i}\rrbracket_{E}+\sum_{i}\,\big(\rho_{E}(k)\cdot f_{i}\big)\,w_{i}\ ,

for each
k\in K
. Since
w_{i}
are basic, it follows that

\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K),\mathsf{\Gamma}(W)\rrbracket_{E}\subseteq\mathsf{\Gamma}(W),

hence
W
is a
K
-transverse generalised metric and
\natural(W)=V^{+}
.

Uniqueness is seen as follows: Suppose there exists another
K
-transverse generalised metric
W^{\prime}
such that
\natural(W^{\prime})=V^{+}
. By Remark
2.47
, it follows that
W^{\prime}
is spanned pointwise by
\set{w^{\prime}_{i}}\subset\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{bas}}(W^{\prime})
. Thus
v_{i}=\natural(w^{\prime}_{i})
give a set of sections spanning
V^{+}
pointwise. Now we apply the previous construction to
v_{i}
to construct
W
.
Hence we get
W
spanned by
w_{i}
with
\natural(w_{i})=\natural(w^{\prime}_{i})
.
Therefore
w_{i}
and
w^{\prime}_{i}
differ by an element in
K
, so
W=W^{\prime}
.

Conversely, starting with a
K
-transverse generalised metric
W
on
E
, then
V^{+}\coloneqq\natural(W)
defines a generalised metric on
\underline{E}
, since the
K
-transversality condition makes
V_{+}
a well-defined subbundle of
\underline{E}
over
{\mathcal{Q}}
by Proposition
2.44
and

(4.47)

\displaystyle\left\langle v,v\right\rangle_{\underline{E}}=\left\langle w,w\right\rangle_{E}\geq 0\ ,

for every
v\in V^{+}
, with equality if and only if
w\in K
, i.e.
v=0
. It also follows that
\mathrm{rk}(V^{+})=\dim({\mathcal{Q}})
.

We now show, in both cases, that
Q(K)
is a regular transverse generalised isometry between
W
and
V^{+}
. Fix
p=(m,\varpi(m))\in\text{gr}(\varpi)\subset M\times{\mathcal{Q}}
. Take arbitrary smooth splittings
\widetilde{W}{}^{\pm}
of the sequence (
4.44
), i.e. a splitting of the short exact sequence of vector bundles

(4.48)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow W^{+}\longrightarrow 0

over
M
, and set
\cal W^{\pm}=\widetilde{W}{}^{\pm}\times V^{\pm}
. Then

(4.49)

\displaystyle Q(K)_{p}\cap(\cal W^{+}\oplus\cal W^{-})_{p}=\set{(e,\natural(e))\,}{\,e\in K_{m}^{\perp}\cap(\widetilde{W}^{+}_{m}\oplus\widetilde{W}^{-}_{m})}\ .

Thus if
(e,\natural(e))\in Q(K)_{p}\cap(\cal W^{+}\oplus\cal W^{-})_{p}
, then

\displaystyle\big(e,\natural(e)\big)=\big(e_{+}+e_{-},\natural(e_{+}+e_{-})\big)\ ,

with
e_{\pm}\in\widetilde{W}_{m}^{\pm}
. By construction
\natural(\widetilde{W}^{\pm})=V^{\pm}
. Hence

\displaystyle\big(e,\natural(e)\big)

\displaystyle=(e_{+}+e_{-},\underline{e}{}_{+}+\underline{e}{}_{-})

\displaystyle=(e_{+},\underline{e}{}_{+})+(e_{-},\underline{e}{}_{-})\ \in\ \big(Q(K)_{p}\cap\cal W^{+}_{p}\big)\oplus\big(Q(K)_{p}\cap\cal W^{-}_{p}\big)\ ,

where
\underline{e}{}_{\pm}=\natural(e_{\pm})=\natural(e)_{\pm}\in V^{\pm}
.
Thus Equation (
4.41
) follows, and
Q(K)
is a regular transverse generalised isometry between
W
and
V^{+}
.
∎

Example 4.50
.

For a twisted standard Courant algebroid
E=(\mathbb{T}M,H)
, a generalised metric on
\underline{E}=(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}},\underline{H})
is given by a metric
{\underline{g}}\in\Gamma(\text{\Large$\odot$}^{2}T^{*}\cal Q)
and a two-form
{\underline{b}}\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{2}(\cal Q).
Then the pullbacks
\varpi^{*}{\underline{g}}
and
\varpi^{*}{\underline{b}}
define a
K
-transverse generalised metric
W
such that
\natural(W)=V^{+}
, and
Q({\mathcal{F}})
is a regular transverse generalised isometry between
W
and
V^{+}
.

4.4.
Composition of Transverse Generalised Isometries

Composition of transverse generalised isometries presents a multi-faceted problem by nature of its less restrictive construction compared to generalised isometries. To elucidate why this is so, suppose
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
and
R^{\prime}\colon E_{2}\dashrightarrow E_{3}
are Courant algebroid relations composing cleanly, and let
W_{i}
be transverse generalised metrics on
E_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
. Suppose further that
R
is a transverse generalised isometry between
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
, and
R^{\prime}
is a transverse generalised isometry between
W_{2}
and
W_{3}
. The problems encountered are two-fold.

Firstly, since the lifts
\widetilde{W}^{\pm}_{i}
of
W^{\pm}_{i}
are not unique (as would be the case for a generalised metric) it may not be the case that the lift
\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\pm}
making
R
a transverse generalised metric is the same as the lift
\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\prime\pm}
making
R^{\prime}
a transverse generalised metric.

Secondly, even if we had
\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\pm}=\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\prime\pm}
, this may still not be enough. For instance, suppose
(e_{1},e_{3})\in(R^{\prime}\circ R)\cap(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\times\widetilde{W}_{3}^{+})
. By definition, there exists
e_{2}
such that
(e_{1},e_{2})\in R
and
(e_{2},e_{3})\in R^{\prime}
. But it is in no way guaranteed that
e_{2}\in\widetilde{W}_{2}^{+}
.

The first problem is discussed in Appendix
A
. For now we assume that
\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\pm}=\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\prime\pm}
and address the second problem through

Proposition 4.51
.

Suppose that
R_{1}\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
and
R_{2}\colon E_{2}\dashrightarrow E_{3}
are Courant algebroid relations supported on
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, respectively, that compose cleanly.
For
i=1,2,3
let
K_{i}
be isotropic involutive subbundles of
E_{i}
, and let
W_{i}
be pre-
K_{i}
-transverse generalised metrics such that
R_{i}
is a transverse generalised isometry between
W_{i}
and
W_{i+1}
. Suppose there are splittings
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{\pm}
,
\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\pm}
and
\widetilde{W}_{3}^{\pm}
giving the decomposition (
4.41
) for
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
. Let
\cal A^{\pm}=\widetilde{W}_{1}^{\pm}\times\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\pm}\times\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\pm}\times\widetilde{W}_{3}^{\pm}
. If

(4.52)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rk}\big((R_{2}\diamond R_{1})_{(c_{1},c_{2})}\cap(\cal A^{+}\oplus\cal A^{-})_{(c_{1},c_{2})}\big)>0\ ,

for every
(c_{1},c_{2})\in C_{2}\diamond C_{1}
, then
R_{2}\circ R_{1}
is a transverse generalised isometry between
W_{1}
and
W_{3}
.

Proof.

Take
(e_{1},e_{3})\in(R_{2}\circ R_{1})\cap(\cal W^{+}\oplus\cal W^{-})
, where
\cal W^{\pm}=\widetilde{W}_{1}^{\pm}\times\widetilde{W}_{3}^{\pm}
. Then there exists
e_{2}\in E_{2}
such that
(e_{i},e_{i+1})\in R_{i}
for
i=1,2
. That is,
(e_{1},e_{2},e_{2},e_{3})\in R_{2}\diamond R_{1}
. By Equation (
4.52
) we may assume
e_{2}=e_{2}^{+}+e_{2}^{-}
where
e^{\pm}_{2}\in\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\pm}
. Then

(4.53)

\displaystyle(e_{i},e_{i+1})

\displaystyle=(e_{i}^{+}+e_{i}^{-},e_{i+1}^{+}+e_{i+1}^{-})

(4.54)

\displaystyle=(e_{i}^{+},e_{i+1}^{+})+(e_{i}^{-},e_{i+1}^{-})\ \in\ R_{i}\cap\big((\widetilde{W}_{i}^{+}\times\widetilde{W}_{i+1}^{+})\oplus(\widetilde{W}_{i}^{-}\times\widetilde{W}_{i+1}^{-})\big)\ ,

for
i=1,2
. Since
R_{i}
is a transverse generalised isometry between
W_{i}
and
W_{i+1}
, the decomposition (
4.41
) holds for
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, hence
(e_{i}^{\pm},e_{i+1}^{\pm})\in R_{i}
. It then follows by definition that
(e_{1}^{\pm},e_{3}^{\pm})\in R_{2}\circ R_{1}
, and so the decomposition (
4.41
) also holds for
R_{2}\circ R_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 4.55
.

If either
R_{1}
or
R_{2}
is the graph of a classical Courant algebroid isomorphism, then
R_{2}\circ R_{1}
is always a transverse generalised isometry between
W_{1}
and
W_{3}
.

Proof.

Suppose
R_{1}=\text{gr}(\Phi)
, where
\Phi\colon E_{1}\to E_{2}
is a Courant algebroid isomorphism over a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
(hence
C_{1}=\text{gr}(\varphi))
. Since
\Phi
is an isomorphism, we have already seen that
\widetilde{W}_{2}^{\pm}=\Phi(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{\pm})
. To show that Equation (
4.52
) holds, fix
(c_{1},c_{2})\in C_{2}\diamond C_{1}
.
Then at this point

(4.56)

\displaystyle R_{2}\diamond\text{gr}(\Phi)=\set{(e_{1},\Phi(e_{1}),\Phi(e_{1}),e_{3})\,}{\,e_{1}\in E_{1}\ ,\ (\Phi(e_{1}),e_{3})\in R_{2}}\ .

But if
e_{1}\in\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\oplus\widetilde{W}_{1}^{-}
then
\Phi(e_{1})\in\widetilde{W}_{2}^{+}\oplus\widetilde{W}_{2}^{-}
, and hence Equation (
4.52
) holds.

If
R_{2}
is the graph of a classical Courant algebroid isomorphism
\Phi
, then this argument can be applied to
(R_{1}\circ\text{gr}(\Phi))^{\top}=\text{gr}(\Phi^{-1})\circ R_{1}^{\top}
, as the transpose of a transverse generalised isometry is a transverse generalised isometry.
∎

5.
T-duality as a Courant Algebroid Relation

Recall that infinitesimal symmetries of the Wess-Zumino functional
S_{H_{1}}
of a string sigma-model are characterised by the Dorfman bracket of the standard Courant algebroid
(\mathbb{T}M_{1},H_{1})
. A Courant algebroid isomorphism thus maps symmetries of
S_{H_{1}}
to symmetries of
S_{H_{2}}
, with
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
related by Equation (
1.6
), hence giving the same equations of motion. This however requires the string backgrounds to be diffeomorphic. T-duality is a correspondence between dynamics arising from sigma-models whose backgrounds are not necessarily diffeomorphic, but whose symmetries are related in a certain sense.
Courant algebroid relations preserve the Dorfman bracket even when the backgrounds are not diffeomorphic. This motivates an attempt to reformulate T-duality in terms of Courant algebroid relations.

In this section we formalise this idea, where the Courant algebroid relation may be viewed as the relation induced by two reductions of an exact Courant algebroid.
That is, we consider an exact Courant algebroid
E
over a manifold
M
and take isotropic subbundles
K_{1}\subset E
and
K_{2}\subset E
, inducing regular foliations by
\rho_{E}(K_{1})
and
\rho_{E}(K_{2})
of
M
, and apply the reduction procedure of Theorem
2.34
. Under the right conditions, the reduced Courant algebroids inherit a Courant algebroid relation from the reductions. In other words, we will explore circumstances under which the Wess-Zumino functionals of our sigma-models are related.

5.1.
Topological T-duality

Let
E
be an exact Courant algebroids over
M
endowed with isotropic subbundles
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
. Here and throughout the rest of the paper we will assume that

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rk}(K_{1})=\mathrm{rk}(K_{2})\ .

Assume as well that
K_{1}^{\perp}
and
K_{2}^{\perp}
have enough basic sections and denote by
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
the foliation of
M
given by the integral manifolds of the distribution
\rho_{E}(K_{i}).
Suppose that the leaf spaces
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=M/{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
have a smooth structure, hence there are unique surjective submersions
\varpi_{i}\colon M\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
for
i=1,2.

From Section
3.2
we can then form the Courant algebroid morphisms
Q(K_{i})\colon E\rightarrowtail\underline{E}{}_{i}
supported on
\text{gr}(\varpi_{i})
. This gives the diagram

(5.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}{}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}{}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(K_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(K_{2})}

which prepares for our first definition of T-duality.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be exact Courant algebroids fitting into the diagram (
5.2
). Then
\underline{E}{}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}
are
T-duality related
over
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
if the composition

(5.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R=Q(K_{2})\circ Q(K_{1})^{\top}\colon\underline{E}{}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}{}_{2}}

is a Courant algebroid relation supported on

(5.5)

\displaystyle C=\set{(\varpi_{1}(m),\varpi_{2}(m))\,}{\,m\in M}\ \subset\ {\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}\ .

The Courant algebroid relation
R
is the
T-duality relation
between
\underline{E}{}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}
.

Remark 5.6
.

We first discuss the smoothness of the supporting submanifold
C
, as in general it may not be smooth. The most interesting case is when
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
are fibred manifolds over a common base manifold
{\mathcal{B}}
. In this case there is the commutative diagram

(5.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

Since the fibred product
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
also fits into the diagram (
5.7
), there is a smooth map
M\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
given by the quotient of
M
by the foliation induced by the distribution
T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
. Thus in this case the rank of
T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
must be constant.
11
11

11

We will see below that this must also be true in the general case without a common base
{\mathcal{B}}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

If
M
fits into the commutative diagram (
5.7
), then
C={\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
is smooth.

Proof.

Note that
C\subseteq{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
. For the opposite inclusion, consider a point
(q_{1},q_{2})
in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
. Then there exist
m,m^{\prime}\in M
such that
q_{1}=\varpi_{1}(m)
and
q_{2}=\varpi_{2}(m^{\prime})
, and
b\coloneqq\pi_{1}(\varpi_{1}(m))=\pi_{2}(\varpi_{2}(m^{\prime}))\in{\mathcal{B}}
. Since also
\pi_{2}(\varpi_{2}(m))=b
, with
q_{0}\coloneqq\varpi_{2}(m)
the points
q_{2}
and
q_{0}
belong to the same fibre of
\pi_{2}
. Hence there is a path
\gamma:[0,1]\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
such that
\gamma(0)=q_{0}
and
\gamma(1)=q_{2}
. By the homotopy lifting property we get a path
\tilde{\gamma}
in
({\mathcal{F}}_{1})_{q_{1}}
such that
\tilde{\gamma}(0)=m
, where
({\mathcal{F}}_{1})_{q_{1}}
is the leaf of the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
given by
\varpi_{1}^{-1}(q_{1})
. We then define
m^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\tilde{\gamma}(1).
It follows that
\varpi_{1}(m^{\prime\prime})=q_{1}
and
\varpi_{2}(m^{\prime\prime})=q_{2}
, hence
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}\subseteq C
.
∎

Assuming
C
is smooth, we can now state the conditions under which the T-duality relation
R
can be formed as

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
E
be an exact Courant algebroid over
M
endowed with isotropic subbundles
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
satisfying the conditions for the diagram (
5.2
) discussed above. Let us also assume that
T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
has constant rank and that
C
is a smooth manifold.
Then
\underline{E}{}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}
are T-duality related if and only if
K_{1}\cap K_{2}
has constant rank.

Proof.

To compose
Q(K_{2})
with
Q(K_{1})^{\top}
, we first check the conditions on the base manifolds.
Setting
C_{1}=\text{gr}(\varpi_{1})
and
C_{2}=\text{gr}(\varpi_{2})
, the intersection of
C_{1}^{\top}\times C_{2}
with
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times\Delta(M)\times{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
is clean: one can show that they intersect transversally
12
12

12

We say that submanifolds
S
and
S^{\prime}
of
M

intersect transversally
if
T_{s}S+T_{s}S^{\prime}=T_{s}M
for each
s\in S\cap S^{\prime}
. Submanifolds that intersect transversally also intersect cleanly
[
42
, Appendix C.3]
.
in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times M\times M\times{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
. For if
v\in T_{\hat{m}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times M\times M\times{\mathcal{Q}}_{2})
, for any
\hat{m}\in(C_{1}^{\top}\times C_{2})\cap({\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times\Delta(M)\times{\mathcal{Q}}_{2})
, then

(5.10)

\displaystyle v=({\underline{v}}_{1},v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime},{\underline{v}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\big((\varpi_{1})_{*}(v_{1}),v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime},(\varpi_{2})_{*}(v_{1}^{\prime})\big)

(5.11)

\displaystyle\quad\,+\big({\underline{v}}_{1}-(\varpi_{1})_{*}(v_{1}),0,0,{\underline{v}}_{2}-(\varpi_{2})_{*}(v_{1}^{\prime})\big)\ ,

where the first term is an element of
T_{\hat{m}}(C_{1}^{\top}\times C_{2})
and the second term is an element of
T_{\hat{m}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times\Delta(M)\times{\mathcal{Q}}_{2})
.
This implies the condition
(ii)
on the base manifolds of Proposition
3.19
. Next since
C=C_{2}\circ C^{\top}_{1}
is a smooth manifold and
T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
has constant rank, the map
C_{2}\diamond C_{1}^{\top}\to C_{2}\circ C_{1}^{\top}
is a smooth surjective submersion, thereby satisfying the condition
(ii)
on the base manifolds of Proposition
3.20
.

To check the constant rank criteria of conditions
(ii)
of Propositions
3.19
and
3.20
, we note that since
Q(K_{1})=\set{(e,\natural_{1}(e))\,}{\,e\in K_{1}^{\perp}}
,

(5.12)

\displaystyle Q(K_{2})\diamond Q(K_{1})^{\top}=\set{(\natural_{1}(e),e,e,\natural_{2}(e))\,}{\,e\in K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}}\ .

This is pointwise isomorphic to
K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}
, and for
m\in M
we find

(5.13)

\displaystyle\dim\big((K_{2}^{\perp})_{m}\cap(K_{1}^{\perp})_{m}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rk}(K_{1}^{\perp})+\mathrm{rk}(K_{2}^{\perp})-\dim\big((K_{1}^{\perp})_{m}+(K_{2}^{\perp})_{m}\big)

(5.14)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rk}(K_{1}^{\perp})+\mathrm{rk}(K_{2}^{\perp})-\mathrm{rk}(E_{2})+\dim\big((K_{1}^{\perp})_{m}^{\perp}\cap(K_{2}^{\perp})_{m}^{\perp}\big)

(5.15)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}^{\perp}\big)+\mathrm{rk}(K_{2}^{\perp})-\mathrm{rk}(E_{2})+\dim\big((K_{1})_{m}\cap(K_{2})_{m}\big)\ .

It follows that condition
(ii)
of Proposition
3.19
is satisfied if and only if the dimension of
(K_{1})_{m}\cap(K_{2})_{m}
is independent of
m\in M
.

Finally, the kernel of the projection of Equation (
5.12
) to
Q(K_{2})\circ Q(K_{1})^{\top}
is
K_{1}\cap K_{2}
. Hence by item
(ii)
of Proposition
3.20
, the composition is clean if and only if
K_{1}\cap K_{2}
has constant rank. Thus by Theorem
3.21
, we get the Courant algebroid relation (
5.4
).
∎

The diagram (
5.2
) now becomes

(5.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}{}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}{}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(K_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(K_{1})^{\top}}

Explicitly, if
\natural_{i}\colon K_{i}^{\perp}\to\bigr(K_{i}^{\perp}/K_{i}\bigl)/{\mathcal{F}}_{i}=\underline{E}{}_{i}
denotes the quotient map for
i=1,\,2
, then

(5.17)

\displaystyle R=\set{(\natural_{1}(e),\natural_{2}(e))\,}{\,e\in K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}}\ .

Note that the vertical arrow on the left of the diagram points upwards, as the composition of the relations does not commute.

As mentioned in Section
1
, there is a notion that the T-duality relation should be Lagrangian. Our relation is consistent with this, through

Proposition 5.18
.

R
is maximally isotropic, hence a Dirac structure in
\underline{E}_{1}\times\overline{\underline{E}}_{2}
.

Proof.

Since
R
is involutive by construction, we only need to show that it is maximally isotropic in
\underline{E}_{1}\times\overline{\underline{E}}_{2}.
Note that, from the standpoint of graded symplectic geometry, this is a consequence of the fact that the composition of two Lagrangian relations is again Lagrangian.

Using Equation (
5.1
), we have

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rk}(\underline{E}_{1}\times\overline{\underline{E}}_{2})=2\,\big(\mathrm{rk}(E)-2\,\mathrm{rk}(K_{1})\big)\ .

We have already seen that
Q(K_{2})\diamond Q(K_{1})^{\top}
is isomorphic to
K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}
pointwise, and that its projection to
R
has kernel
K_{1}\cap K_{2}
. Thus

(5.20)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rk}(R)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}\big)-\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}\cap K_{2}\big)=\mathrm{rk}(K_{1}^{\perp})+\mathrm{rk}(K_{2}^{\perp})-\mathrm{rk}(E)=\mathrm{rk}(E)-2\,\mathrm{rk}(K_{1})

as required.
∎

Remark 5.21

(
Bisubmersions
)
.

In the picture of T-duality related Courant algebroids of Definition
5.3
, at the level of base manifolds there are surjective submersions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{1}}

with the fibres of
\varpi_{1}
given by the leaves of the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and the fibres of
\varpi_{2}
given by the leaves of the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}.

If both surjective submersions have connected fibres and the Lie bracket of vector fields satisfies

(5.22)

\displaystyle\big[\mathsf{\Gamma}\bigl(\ker(\varpi_{1*})\bigr),\mathsf{\Gamma}\big(\ker(\varpi_{2*})\big)\big]\subset\mathsf{\Gamma}\bigl(\ker(\varpi_{1*})\bigr)+\mathsf{\Gamma}\bigl(\ker(\varpi_{2*})\bigr)\ ,

then there exist unique (possibly singular) foliations
\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}{}_{1}
on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
and
\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}{}_{2}
on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
such that
[
43
, Corollary 2.16]

(5.23)

\displaystyle\varpi_{1*}^{-1}(T{\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}}{}_{1})=(\varpi_{2})_{*}^{-1}(T{\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}}{}_{2})=\ker(\varpi_{1*})+\ker(\varpi_{2*})\ .

This means that there is a
bisubmersion
between
({\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}{}_{1})
and
({\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}{}_{2})
; see e.g.
[
43
]
and references therein for the general definition of bisubmersion.

This construction is an example of
Hausdorff Morita equivalent foliations
in the sense of
[
43
, Definition 2.1]
. Equation (
5.22
) is an involutivity condition for the subbundle

(5.24)

\displaystyle\ker(\varpi_{1*})+\ker(\varpi_{2*})=T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}+T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\ ,

hence
M
is endowed with a (possibly singular) foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
that induces the Hausdorff Morita equivalent foliations. This is a regular foliation if
\ker(\varpi_{1*})\cap\ker(\varpi_{2*})
has constant rank. This notion will be crucial in the description of the symmetries of the background fields defined on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
, and we will see how it naturally arises in the construction of T-duality relations.

Remark 5.25
.

Courant algebroid reduction is reformulated and extended in the language of graded symplectic reduction by
[
44
]
. It would be interesting to fit our perspective on T-duality, and more generally Courant algebroid relations, into this framework, particularly in light of the differential graded symplectic geometry approach to generalised T-duality taken in
[
25
]
.

Example 5.26
.

Our main source of examples will be the following set up: let
E_{1}=(\mathbb{T}M_{1},H_{1})
and
E_{2}=(\mathbb{T}M_{2},H_{2})
be isomorphic twisted standard Courant algebroids, isomorphic via
\overline{\varphi}\circ{\,\rm e}\,^{B}\,
, where
\overline{\varphi}=\varphi_{*}+(\varphi^{-1})^{*}
and
B\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{2}(M_{1})
with
\mathrm{d}B=H_{1}-\varphi^{*}H_{2}
, as described in Proposition
2.25
.
Suppose that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are foliated by
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{2}
, respectively, such that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}=M_{1}/{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
with quotient map
\varpi_{1}
.
13
13

13

In general we do not require that
\varphi_{*}T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}=T{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Suppose further that
\iota_{X_{1}}H_{1}=0
, for all
X_{1}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{1})
and
\iota_{X_{2}}H_{2}=0
, for all
X_{2}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(T{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{2})
. Finally, let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}=M_{1}/{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
with
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\varphi^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{2})
and quotient map
\varpi_{2}
, and let
\underline{H}_{1}
and
\underline{H}_{2}
be such that
\varpi_{1}^{*}\underline{H}_{1}=H_{1}
,
\varpi_{2}^{*}\underline{H}_{2}=\varphi^{*}H_{2}
. Then

Proposition 5.27
.

If
T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
and
\ker\left(B|_{T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}}\right)
have constant rank,
(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\,{\underline{H}}{}_{1})
and
(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\,{\underline{H}}{}_{2})
are T-duality related.

Proof.

Take
K_{1}=T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
, and
K_{2}={\,\rm e}\,^{-B}\left(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\right)
.
Then

\displaystyle K_{1}\cap K_{2}=\set{v_{1}\in T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\,}{\,\iota_{v_{1}}B=0}=\ker\big(B|_{T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}}\big)\ .

By Corollary
3.15
, there are Courant algebroid relations
Q(K_{1})\colon(\mathbb{T}M_{1},H_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1})
and
Q(K_{2})\colon(\mathbb{T}M_{1},H_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2}).

By Theorem
5.9
,
R=Q(K_{2})\circ Q(K_{1})^{\top}
is a Courant algebroid relation.
∎

5.2.
Geometric T-duality

We now introduce geometric data into our picture of T-duality, which amounts to incorporating the Polyakov functionals (
1.1
) into our string sigma-models; the symmetries of
S_{0}
are generated by Killing vectors of the metric
g
. We will make the following assumptions.
Let
E
be an exact Courant algebroid over
M
endowed with involutive isotropic subbundles
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
that have the same rank. Suppose they satisfy the conditions to fit the diagram (
5.2
) and suppose that the reduced Courant algebroids
\underline{E}{}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}
are T-duality related, i.e. the constant rank assumption of Theorem
5.9
holds. Denote the resulting T-duality relation by
R
. We will work under these assumptions for the remainder of this section.

We begin by endowing the reduced Courant algebroids with generalised metrics.

Definition 5.28
.

The reduced Courant algebroids
(\underline{E}{}_{1},V_{1}^{+})
and
(\underline{E}{}_{2},V_{2}^{+})
are
geometrically T-dual
if
R
is a generalised isometry between the generalised metrics
V_{1}^{+}
and
V_{2}^{+}
.

The starting point of any T-duality transformation is the identification of the symmetries of the original background.

Definition 5.29
.

Let
\underline{E}{}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}
be T-duality related Courant algebroids in the sense of Definition
5.3
and suppose that the unique surjective submersions
\varpi_{i}\colon M\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
satisfy the involutivity condition (
5.22
) discussed in Remark
5.21
, for
i=1,\,2
. The subbundle
D_{1}\coloneqq T{\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}}{}_{1}=\varpi_{1*}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
of
T{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
is the
distribution of T-duality directions
.
14
14

14

This definition can be adapted to the case where
{\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}}{}_{1}
is a singular foliation. Then we consider the T-duality directions to be defined by the locally finitely generated
C^{\infty}_{\rm c}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1})
-module
\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\rm c}(D_{1})
of compactly supported vector fields integrating to
{\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}}{}_{1}.
We do not explicitly discuss this in the following in order to avoid further technicalities. Invariance with respect to a singular foliation relates to the work of Kotov-Strobl
[
45
]
.

Since we may form the relations
Q(K_{i})
for
i=1,2
, by Remark
2.56
there exists splittings
\sigma_{i}\colon TM\to E
of the short exact sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{i}}

which are adapted to
K_{i}
, where
\sigma^{*}_{i}\coloneqq\sigma_{i}^{\rm t}\circ\flat_{E}
is the induced left splitting and
\flat_{E}\colon E\to E^{*}
is the isomorphism induced by the pairing. By Remark
2.50
,
\sigma_{i}
induces a splitting
\underline{\sigma}_{i}
of
\underline{E}_{i}
, giving also a left splitting
\underline{\sigma}^{*}_{i}
.

We are now ready to relate the distribution
D_{1}
to the symmetries of our initial background and begin to justify our terminology ‘T-duality directions’.

Remark 5.30
.

Let us discuss how we can state an invariance condition of a generalised metric solely involving the tensors
(\underline{g},\underline{B})
. Let
{\underline{\sigma}}_{1}\colon T{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\to\underline{E}_{1}
be a splitting of
\underline{E}_{1}
descending from an adapted splitting
\sigma_{1}
of
E
, then
V_{1}^{+}
corresponds to a pair
({\underline{g}}{}_{1},{\underline{b}}{}_{1})
under the isomorphism
(\underline{\sigma}_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho^{*}_{\underline{E}_{1}})^{-1}\colon\underline{E}_{1}\to\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
, i.e. any element
{\underline{w}}{}_{1}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(V_{1}^{+})
can be written as

(5.31)

\displaystyle{\underline{w}}{}_{1}={\underline{Y}}+\iota_{\underline{Y}}(\underline{g}{}_{1}+\underline{b}{}_{1})\ ,

for some
\underline{Y}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(T{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}).

Let
\underline{X}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(D_{1})
. Then the generalised metric
(\underline{g}_{1},\underline{b}_{1})
is invariant with respect to
\underline{X}
if

(5.32)

\displaystyle\pounds_{\underline{X}}(\underline{g}{}_{1}+\underline{b}{}_{1})=0\ .

Note that we can write

(5.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\llbracket\underline{X},\underline{w}_{1}\rrbracket_{\underline{H}{}_{1}}&=[\underline{X},\underline{Y}]+\iota_{\underline{Y}}(\pounds_{\underline{X}}(\underline{g}{}_{1}+\underline{b}{}_{1}))+\iota_{[\underline{X},\underline{Y}]}(\underline{g}{}_{1}+\underline{b}{}_{1})+{\rm pr}_{2}(\llbracket\underline{X},\rho(\underline{w}_{1})\rrbracket_{\underline{H}{}_{1}})\ ,\end{split}

where
{\rm pr}_{2}(\llbracket\underline{X},\underline{Y}\rrbracket_{\underline{H}{}_{1}})=\iota_{\underline{X}}\iota_{\underline{Y}}\underline{H}
.
Hence by imposing the condition (
5.32
), we obtain

(5.34)

\displaystyle\llbracket\underline{X},\underline{w}_{1}\rrbracket_{\underline{H}{}_{1}}-{\rm pr}_{2}(\llbracket\underline{X},\rho(\underline{w}_{1})\rrbracket_{\underline{H}{}_{1}})\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\text{gr}(\underline{g}_{1}+\underline{b}_{1}))\,,

and vice versa.

Condition (
5.34
) can be stated for a generalised metric
V_{1}^{+}
on
\underline{E}_{1}
without making reference to the isomorphism
(\underline{\sigma}_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho^{*}_{\underline{E}_{1}})^{-1}
as follows.

Definition 5.35
.

Let
\underline{\sigma}_{1}\colon T{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\to\underline{E}{}_{1}
be the splitting of the exact Courant algebroid
\underline{E}{}_{1}
over
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
induced by an adapted splitting
\sigma_{1}\colon TM\to E
of
E
.
The generalised metric
V_{1}^{+}
on
\underline{E}{}_{1}
is
invariant with respect to
D_{1}
, or
D_{1}
-invariant
, if there exists a Lie subalgebra
\mathsf{Iso}(V^{+}_{1})
of
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(D_{1})
which spans
D_{1}
pointwise, and is such that

(5.36)

\displaystyle\llbracket\underline{\sigma}_{1}(\underline{X}),\underline{w}{}_{1}\rrbracket_{\underline{E}{}_{1}}-{\rho}{}_{\underline{E}{}_{1}}^{*}\bigl({\underline{\sigma}}^{*}_{1}(\llbracket\underline{\sigma}_{1}(\underline{X}),\underline{\sigma}_{1}(\rho{}_{\underline{E}{}_{1}}(\underline{w}{}_{1}))\rrbracket_{\underline{E}{}_{1}})\bigr)\ \in\ \mathsf{\Gamma}(V_{1}^{+})

for every
{\underline{X}}\in\mathsf{Iso}(V^{+}_{1})
and
\underline{w}{}_{1}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(V_{1}^{+}).
The subalgebra
\mathsf{Iso}(V^{+}_{1})
is called the
isometry subalgebra
of
D_{1}
.

Remark 5.37
.

On an exact Courant algebroid
E
over
M
with Ševera class
[H]
and an isotropic splitting
\sigma\colon TM\to E
, for
X,Y,Z\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(TM)
one has

(5.38)

\displaystyle H(X,Y,Z)=\left\langle\llbracket\sigma(X),\sigma(Y)\rrbracket_{E},\sigma(Z)\right\rangle_{E}

\displaystyle=\iota_{Z}\,\sigma^{*}(\llbracket\sigma(X),\sigma(Y)\rrbracket_{E})

(5.39)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\rho_{E}^{*}(\sigma^{*}(\llbracket\sigma(X),\sigma(Y)\rrbracket_{E})),\sigma(Z)\right\rangle_{E}\ .

Hence the condition (
5.36
) has the interpretation of an invariance condition which itself imposes no restrictions on the representative
H
of the Ševera class; Remark
5.42
below makes this point clearer.
Recall that a subbundle
W
is invariant with respect to an involutive subbundle
K
if satisfies
\llbracket{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(K),{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(W)\rrbracket_{E}\subset{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(W)
, which implies
\iota_{X}H=0,
for all
X\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\rho(K))
, see
[
34
]
.
Definition
5.35
is formulated in a way that avoids imposing any conditions on the representative
H
of the Ševera class of
E
.

Next we introduce a notion of when the adapted splittings are compatible.

Definition 5.40
.

Let us assume we have a
D_{1}
-invariant generalised metric
V_{1}^{+}
on
\underline{E}_{1}
, with isometry subalgebra
\mathsf{Iso}(V^{+}_{1})
. The adapted splittings
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
to
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
, respectively, are
compatible
if

(5.41)

\displaystyle\llbracket(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})(X),e\rrbracket_{E}=\rho_{E}^{*}\bigl(\sigma^{*}_{1}(\llbracket\sigma_{1}(X),\sigma_{1}(\rho_{E}(e))\rrbracket_{E})\bigr)

for every
X\in(\varpi_{1})_{*}^{-1}(\mathsf{Iso}(V_{1}^{+}))\cap\mathsf{\Gamma}(\rho_{E}(K_{2}))\eqqcolon\mathsf{Iso}(W_{1})
and
e\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)
.

Remark 5.42
.

Let
\sigma_{1}\colon TM\to E
be an adapted splitting to
K_{1}
. Then, under the isomorphism
(\sigma_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{E}^{*})^{-1}\colon E\to\mathbb{T}M
, the image of
K_{1}
is
T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
. Moreover,
\sigma_{1}
composed with the isomorphism is the inclusion of
TM
into
\mathbb{T}M
, and yields a representative
H_{\sigma_{1}}
of the Ševera class of
E
. Meanwhile, an adapted splitting
\sigma_{2}
for
K_{2}
induces
H_{\sigma_{2}}
.
The difference is given by

(5.43)

\displaystyle(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2})(X)=\rho_{E}^{*}(\iota_{X}B)

with
H_{\sigma_{1}}-H_{\sigma_{2}}=\mathrm{d}B
. The adapted splittings
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are compatible if

(5.44)

\displaystyle\llbracket\iota_{X}B,Y+\xi\rrbracket_{H_{\sigma_{1}}}-{\rm pr}_{2}(\llbracket X,Y\rrbracket_{H_{\sigma_{1}}})

\displaystyle=-\iota_{Y}\,\mathrm{d}\,\iota_{X}B-\iota_{Y}\,\iota_{X}H_{\sigma_{1}}

(5.45)

\displaystyle=-\iota_{Y}\,\mathrm{d}\,\iota_{X}B-\iota_{Y}\,\iota_{X}\,\mathrm{d}B=-\iota_{Y}\,\pounds_{X}B\ ,

for all
Y+\xi\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\mathbb{T}M_{1}),
where we use that
\iota_{X}H_{\sigma_{2}}=\iota_{X}(H_{\sigma_{1}}-\mathrm{d}B)=0
for the second equality. Thus the splittings are compatible if and only if
\pounds_{X}B=0
for every
X\in\mathsf{Iso}(W_{1})
.

We are now ready to state our main result about the existence and uniqueness of geometrically T-dual backgrounds in the sense of Definition
5.28
through

Theorem 5.46
.

Let
E
be an exact Courant algebroids over
M
. Suppose
E
is endowed with involutive isotropic subbundles
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
of the same rank giving T-duality related Courant algebroids
\underline{E}{}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}
with T-duality relation
R
. Assume further that the bisubmersion involutivity condition (
5.22
) holds, and that there are adapted splittings
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
of
E
which are compatible. Finally, assume that
\underline{E}{}_{1}
is endowed with a
D_{1}
-invariant generalised metric
V_{1}^{+}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

K_{1}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}\subseteq K_{2}\ .

(ii)

K_{2}\cap K_{1}^{\perp}\subseteq K_{1}\ .

(iii)

There exists a unique generalised metric
V_{2}^{+}
on
\underline{E}{}_{2}
such that
R
is a generalised isometry between
V_{1}^{+}
and
V_{2}^{+}
, i.e.
(\underline{E}{}_{1},V_{1}^{+})
and
(\underline{E}{}_{2},V_{2}^{+})
are geometrically T-dual.

Remark 5.47
.

We split the proof in three parts, beginning with Lemma
5.49
below which establishes the implications

(5.48)

\displaystyle\mathrm{\ref{item:main1}}\Longleftrightarrow\mathrm{\ref{item:main2}}\Longleftarrow\mathrm{\ref{item:main3}}\ .

The final implication requires the construction of the generalised metric
V_{2}^{+}
on
\underline{E}{}_{2}
. The first step in this construction is to show that one can lift
V_{1}^{+}
to
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\subset K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}
. Proposition
5.56
below establishes this result.
The remainder of the proof is devoted to showing that the induced pre-
K_{2}
-transverse generalised metric
W_{2}\coloneqq K_{2}\oplus\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}
is a
K_{2}
-transverse generalised metric, and hence reduces to a generalised metric
V_{2}^{+}
.

Lemma 5.49
.

Suppose that
E
is an exact Courant algebroid over
M
such that
\underline{E}{}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}
are T-duality related with T-duality relation
R
. Then the implications (
5.48
) hold.

Proof.

To show that
(i)
implies
(ii)
, we note that

(5.50)

\displaystyle K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}\subseteq K_{2}\implies K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}\subseteq K_{1}\cap K_{2}\ .

Since
K_{2}^{\perp}\supset K_{2}
, it follows that

(5.51)

\displaystyle K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}=K_{2}\cap K_{1}\ .

By Theorem
5.9
,
K_{2}\cap K_{1}
has constant rank.

Using
\mathrm{rk}(K_{1})=\mathrm{rk}(K_{2})
(condition (
5.1
)), we then obtain

(5.52)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}\big)+\mathrm{rk}(K_{2}^{\perp})-\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}+K_{2}^{\perp}\big)

(5.53)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{2}\big)+\mathrm{rk}(K_{2}^{\perp})-\big(\mathrm{rk}(E_{2})-\mathrm{rk}(K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2})\big)

(5.54)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rk}(E_{2})-\mathrm{rk}(E_{2})+\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2})\big)=\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2})\big)\ .

Hence

(5.55)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}\cap K_{2}\big)=\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}\big)=\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}\big)\ .

One always has
K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}\supseteq K_{1}\cap K_{2}
. Since their ranks are the same, this becomes an equality. In particular, we obtain condition
(ii)
.

The converse implication
(ii)
\implies
(i)
follows from the same argument.

Finally, suppose that
(i)
does not hold. Then there is an element
k_{1}\in K_{1}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}
such that
k_{1}\notin K_{2}
. Then by Proposition
4.25
,
R
cannot be a generalised isometry.
∎

Proposition 5.56
.

Suppose that
E
is an exact Courant algebroid over
M
endowed with involutive isotropic subbundles
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
of the same rank, such that
\underline{E}{}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}
are T-duality related.
If either of the equivalent conditions
(i)
or
(ii)
of Theorem
5.46
holds,
then there exists a splitting
s_{0}\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\to K_{1}^{\perp}
of the short exact sequence

(5.57)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow K_{1}\longrightarrow K_{1}^{\perp}\longrightarrow K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\longrightarrow 0

such that
\mathrm{im}(s_{0})\subseteq K_{2}^{\perp}
, which is unique up to elements of
K_{1}\cap K_{2}
.

Proof.

Take an arbitrary splitting
s\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\to K_{1}^{\perp}
of the short exact sequence (
5.57
). If
\mathrm{im}(s)\nsubseteq K_{2}^{\perp}
, then there exists
[e]\in K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}
and
k_{2}\in K_{2}
such that
\left\langle s([e]),k_{2}\right\rangle_{E}\neq 0
. We define the vector bundle map
\beta_{s}
over the diffeomorphism
\varphi
by

(5.58)

\displaystyle\beta_{s}=\flat_{E}\circ s\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\longrightarrow K_{2}^{*}\ ,

where
\flat_{E}\colon E\to E^{*}
is the isomorphism induced by the pairing
\left\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle_{E}
on
E
:

(5.59)

\displaystyle\iota_{k_{2}}\,\beta_{s}([e_{1}])\coloneqq\left\langle s([e_{1}]),k_{2}\right\rangle_{E}\ ,

for
[e_{1}]\in K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}
and
k_{2}\in K_{2}
.

If
k_{2}\in K_{2}\cap K_{1}
, then

(5.60)

\displaystyle\left\langle s([e_{1}]),k_{2}\right\rangle_{E}=0

since
s([e_{1}])\in K_{1}^{\perp}
. Thus we can further define a map
\bar{\beta}_{0}
by quotienting out this subspace:

(5.61)

\displaystyle\bar{\beta}_{0}\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\longrightarrow\big(K_{2}/(K_{2}\cap K_{1})\big)^{*}\ ,\qquad\iota_{[k_{2}]}\bar{\beta}_{0}([e_{1}])\coloneqq\left\langle s([e_{1}]),k_{2}\right\rangle_{E}\ ,

for
[e_{1}]\in K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}
and
[k_{2}]\in K_{2}/(K_{2}\cap K_{1})
.

Similarly we define

(5.62)

\displaystyle\bar{\beta}\colon K_{1}\longrightarrow\big(K_{2}/(K_{2}\cap K_{1})\big)^{*}\ ,\qquad\iota_{[k_{2}]}\bar{\beta}(k_{1})\coloneqq\left\langle k_{1},k_{2}\right\rangle_{E}\ ,

for
k_{1}\in K_{1}
and
[k_{2}]\in K_{2}/(K_{2}\cap K_{1})
, which is again well-defined.

We show that
\bar{\beta}
is surjective: the kernel of
\bar{\beta}
is given by

(5.63)

\displaystyle\ker(\bar{\beta})=\set{k_{1}\in K_{1}\,}{\,\left\langle k_{1},k_{2}\right\rangle_{E}=0\ \text{for all}\ k_{2}\in K_{2}}=K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}\ .

By assumption,
K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}\subseteq K_{2}
, hence
15
15

15

See the start of the proof of Lemma
5.49
.

(5.64)

\displaystyle\ker(\bar{\beta})=K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}=K_{2}\cap K_{1}\ .

Recalling that
K_{2}\cap K_{1}
has constant rank and that
\mathrm{rk}(K_{1})=\mathrm{rk}(K_{2})
, it thus follows that

(5.65)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rk}(\bar{\beta})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rk}(K_{1})-\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{2}\cap K_{1}\big)

(5.66)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rk}(K_{2})-\mathrm{rk}\big(K_{1}\cap K_{2}\big)=\mathrm{rk}\big((K_{2}/(K_{2}\cap K_{1}))^{*}\big)\ .

Hence
\bar{\beta}
is onto, and we can find a map
\alpha\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\to K_{1}
which fits into the diagram

(5.67)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(K_{2}/(K_{2}\cap K_{1})\big)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\beta}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\beta}}

We can now define a new splitting
s_{0}\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\to K_{1}^{\perp}
of the short exact sequence (
5.57
) by

(5.68)

\displaystyle s_{0}\coloneqq s-\alpha\ .

It follows that
\text{im}(s_{0})\subseteq K_{2}^{\perp}
, since for every
k_{2}\in K_{2}
and
[e_{1}]\in K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}
we can compute

(5.69)

\displaystyle\left\langle s_{0}([e_{1}]),k_{2}\right\rangle_{E}

\displaystyle=\left\langle s([e_{1}]),k_{2}\right\rangle_{E}-\left\langle\alpha([e_{1}]),k_{2}\right\rangle_{E}

(5.70)

\displaystyle=\iota_{[k_{2}]}\bar{\beta}_{0}([e_{1}])-\iota_{[k_{2}]}\bar{\beta}(\alpha([e_{1}]))

(5.71)

\displaystyle=\iota_{[k_{2}]}\bar{\beta}_{0}([e_{1}])-\iota_{[k_{2}]}\bar{\beta}_{0}([e_{1}])=0\ .

Hence the new splitting is a map
s_{0}\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\to K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}^{\perp}
.
To show uniqueness up to elements of
K_{2}\cap K_{1}
, note that there are two sources of non-uniqueness for
s_{0}
: one from the choice of the initial splitting
s\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\to K_{1}^{\perp}
, and one from the choice of
\alpha
fitting the diagram (
5.67
).

Since the map
\alpha
is given by the right inverse of
\bar{\beta}
, it is unique up to elements of the kernel of
\bar{\beta}
, which by Equation (
5.63
) is
K_{2}\cap K_{1}
.
That is, if
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
close the diagram (
5.67
), then there are induced splittings
s_{0}
and
s_{0}^{\prime}
respectively defined by Equation (
5.68
), and

(5.72)

\displaystyle s_{0}-s_{0}^{\prime}=\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\longrightarrow K_{2}\cap K_{1}\ .

Finally, suppose we started with different splittings
s,s^{\prime}\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\to K_{1}^{\perp}
. Using the above construction, we obtain splittings
s_{0},s_{0}^{\prime}\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\to K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}^{\perp}
. These splittings differ by elements of
K_{1}
, hence

(5.73)

\displaystyle s_{0}-s_{0}^{\prime}\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\longrightarrow K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}=K_{1}\cap K_{2}\ ,

giving the required uniqueness.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.46
.

Assume that condition
(i)
of Theorem
5.46
holds.
By Theorem
4.45
, there is a unique
K_{1}
-transverse generalised metric
W_{1}
on
E
such that
Q(K_{1})
is a regular transverse generalised isometry between
W_{1}
and
V_{1}^{+}
, using an arbitrary lift
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}
of
W_{1}^{+}=W_{1}/K_{1}
to
E
. From Proposition
5.56
it follows that we can choose
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}
such that
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\subseteq K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}^{\perp}
. Set
\widetilde{W}_{2}^{+}\coloneqq\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\subseteq K_{2}^{\perp}
. By the uniqueness of the splitting
s\colon K_{1}^{\perp}/K_{1}\to K_{1}^{\perp}
up to elements in
K_{1}\cap K_{2}
, this choice of
\widetilde{W}_{2}^{+}
is unique up to elements of
K_{1}\cap K_{2}
.

Thus we may uniquely define the bundle

(5.74)

\displaystyle W_{2}=\widetilde{W}_{2}^{+}\oplus K_{2}\ .

The sum is direct: if
k_{2}\in\widetilde{W}_{2}^{+}\cap K_{2}
, then
k_{2}\in\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}
. Since
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\cap K_{1}=\set{0}
, it follows that
k_{2}\notin K_{1}
. But this contradicts condition
(ii)
unless
k_{2}=0
.

It follows that
W_{2}
is a pre-
K_{2}
-transverse generalised metric on
E
. This can be seen by noting firstly that
K_{2}\subset W_{2}\subset K_{2}^{\perp}
, since
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\subset K_{2}^{\perp}
. Moreover, for every
w_{2}\in W_{2}
such that
w_{2}\notin K_{2}
,
it follows that
w_{2}\in\widetilde{W}_{2}^{+}
.
It then follows that
\left\langle w_{2},w_{2}\right\rangle_{E}>0
.

From Theorem
4.45
it follows that
Q(K_{1})^{\top}
is a transverse generalised isometry between
V_{1}^{+}
and
W_{2}
.

We shall now show that
W_{2}
is a
K_{2}
-transverse generalised metric which descends to the quotient. Since
V_{1}^{+}
is
D_{1}
-invariant, for every
\underline{w}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(V_{1}^{+})
and
\underline{X}\in\mathsf{Iso}(V_{1}^{+})
it follows by definition that

(5.75)

\displaystyle\llbracket\underline{\sigma}_{1}(\underline{X}),\underline{w}_{1}\rrbracket_{\underline{E}{}_{1}}-{\rho}{}_{\underline{E}_{1}}^{*}\bigl({\underline{\sigma}}^{*}_{1}(\llbracket\underline{\sigma}_{1}(\underline{X}),\underline{\sigma}_{1}(\rho{}_{\underline{E}_{1}}(\underline{w}_{1}))\rrbracket_{\underline{E}{}_{1}})\bigr)\ \in\ \mathsf{\Gamma}(V_{1}^{+})\ .

Take a subset of sections
\set{\underline{w}{}_{j}}
spanning
V_{1}^{+}
pointwise and
\set{\underline{X}_{i}}\subset\mathsf{Iso}(V^{+}_{1})
spanning
D_{1}
pointwise. The former lifts to a set of basic sections
\set{w_{j}}
spanning
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}
pointwise, the latter lifts to a set of projectable vector fields
\set{X_{i}}\subset\mathsf{Iso}(W_{1})
spanning
T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
pointwise.
Since
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are compatible, it follows that

(5.76)

\displaystyle\llbracket\sigma_{2}(X_{i}),w_{j}\rrbracket_{E}=\llbracket\sigma_{1}(X_{i}),w_{j}\rrbracket_{E}-\rho_{E}^{*}\bigl(\sigma^{*}_{1}(\llbracket\sigma_{1}(X_{i}),\sigma_{1}(\rho_{E}(w_{j}))\rrbracket_{E})\bigr)\ .

If
X\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(TM_{1})
is projectable, then
\sigma_{1}(X)
is a basic section, since
\sigma_{1}
is an adapated splitting.
Thus since the sections
\sigma(X_{i})
and
w_{j}
are basic, the right-hand side of Equation (
5.76
) is basic
16
16

16

Recall that
\llbracket{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(K_{1}^{\perp}),{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(K_{1}^{\perp})\rrbracket_{E_{1}}\subset{\mathsf{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{bas}}(K_{1}^{\perp})
.
and descends to the quotient; by Equation (
5.75
), it moreover descends to a section of
V_{1}^{+}
.
It follows that the right-hand side of Equation (
5.76
) is a section of
W_{1}
, hence
\llbracket\sigma_{2}(X_{i}),w_{j}\rrbracket_{E}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+})\oplus{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(K_{1})
.

We next show that in fact
\llbracket\sigma_{2}(X_{i}),w_{j}\rrbracket_{E}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\oplus K_{1}\cap K_{2})
. Suppose that
\llbracket\sigma_{2}(X_{i}),w_{j}\rrbracket_{E}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(K_{1})
. Recall that
X_{i}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(\rho_{E}(K_{2}))

and
\sigma_{2}
is the adapted splitting to
K_{2}
. Therefore
\sigma_{2}(X_{i})\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2})
, and since
w_{j}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}^{\perp})
it follows from Equation (
2.32
) that

(5.77)

\displaystyle\llbracket\sigma_{2}(X_{i}),w_{j}\rrbracket_{E}\ \in\ \mathsf{\Gamma}\big(K_{2}^{\perp}\cap K_{1}\big)=\mathsf{\Gamma}\big(K_{1}\cap K_{2}\big)\ ,

with the last equality following from condition
(i)
. We must therefore have

(5.78)

\displaystyle\llbracket\sigma_{2}(X_{i}),w_{j}\rrbracket_{E}\ \in\ {\mathsf{\Gamma}}\big(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\oplus(K_{1}\cap K_{2})\big)\ .

The final piece of the puzzle is found by noting that, since
\rho_{E}
is injective on
K_{2}
as shown in Lemma
2.52
, it follows that
\set{\sigma_{2}(X_{i})}
is a set of sections spanning
K_{2}
pointwise. Let us denote these sections by
k_{i}\coloneqq\sigma_{2}(X_{i})
. Thus for any
k^{\prime}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2})
and
w\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+})
there are expansions
k^{\prime}=\sum_{i}\,f_{i}\,k_{i}
and
w=\sum_{j}\,h_{j}\,w_{j}
for some functions
f_{i},h_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that the sums are locally finite.

Using the anchored Leibniz rule (
2.4
) and Equation (
2.9
) we may then write

(5.79)

\displaystyle\llbracket k^{\prime},w\rrbracket_{E}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\,\Big(f_{i}\,h_{j}\,\llbracket k_{i},w_{j}\rrbracket_{E}-h_{j}\,\big(\rho_{E}(w_{j})\cdot f_{i}\big)\,k_{i}

(5.80)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\big(\rho_{E}(f_{i}\,k_{i})\cdot h_{j}\big)\,w_{j}+h_{j}\,\left\langle k_{i},w_{j}\right\rangle_{E}\,{\mathcal{D}}_{E}f_{i}\Big)\ .

The first term lives in
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\big(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\oplus(K_{1}\cap K_{2})\big)
by Equation (
5.78
), the second term in
\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2})
, the third term in
\mathsf{\Gamma}(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+})
, and the final term is zero since
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+}\subset K_{2}^{\perp}
.
Hence

(5.81)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2}),\mathsf{\Gamma}(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+})\rrbracket_{E}\ \subset\ \mathsf{\Gamma}(\widetilde{W}_{1}^{+})+\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2})\ .

Thus

(5.82)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2}),\mathsf{\Gamma}(W_{2})\rrbracket_{E}&=\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2}),\mathsf{\Gamma}(\widetilde{W}_{2}^{+})\rrbracket_{E}+\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2}),\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2})\rrbracket_{E}\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\subset\ \mathsf{\Gamma}(\widetilde{W}_{2}^{+})+\mathsf{\Gamma}(K_{2})=\mathsf{\Gamma}(W_{2})\ ,\end{split}

where we have also used the property that
K_{2}
is involutive.

Equation (
5.82
) shows that
W_{2}
is
K_{2}
-invariant, so by Proposition
2.44
it descends to a subbundle
V_{2}^{+}\coloneqq\natural_{2}(W_{2})
of
\underline{E}{}_{2}.

We know from Theorem
4.45
that
V_{2}^{+}
defines a generalised metric on
\underline{E}{}_{2}
such that
Q(K_{2})
is a regular transverse generalised isometry between
W_{2}
and
V_{2}^{+}
. Finally, since

(5.83)

\displaystyle Q(K_{2})\diamond Q(K_{1})^{\top}=\set{(\natural_{1}(e),e,e,\natural_{2}(e))\,}{\,e\in K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}}\ ,

and since
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{\pm}\subseteq K_{1}^{\perp}\cap K_{2}^{\perp}
, the rank condition (
4.52
) is satisfied. It follows that
Q(K_{2})\circ Q(K_{1})^{\top}=R
is a generalised isometry between
V_{1}^{+}
and
V_{2}^{+}
.
Thus
(\underline{E}_{1},V_{1}^{+})
and
(\underline{E}_{2},V_{2}^{+})
are geometrically T-dual.
Uniqueness of
V_{2}^{+}
follows by Corollary
4.28
.
∎

Remark 5.84

(
Classification of T-duality
)
.

In Theorem
5.46
we are able to construct geometrically T-dual backgrounds because of the bisubmersion condition (
5.22
). This leads us to speculate that geometrically T-dual Courant algebroids can only be considered over Hausdorff Morita equivalent foliated manifolds, i.e. they might represent a subset of this equivalence class. This may lead to a classification of T-dual backgrounds.

Remark 5.85
.

So far we have not discussed the dilaton field of the string background.
In order to include the dilaton in our picture, similarly to
[
46
,
12
]
, we should discuss how divergence operators on Courant algebroids behave under transverse generalised isometries. We defer this to future work.

5.2.1.
Geometric T-duality for Standard Courant Algebroids

We shall now discuss Theorem
5.46
when choosing an isomorphism of
E
with
\mathbb{T}M
. Recall that, in the assumptions of
5.46
, we have two compatible adapted splittings
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
. Let
\Psi_{1}=\sigma_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{E}^{*}
be the isomorphims between
\mathbb{T}M
and
E
induced by
\sigma_{1}.
Hence,
\Psi_{1}^{-1}(K_{1})=T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
. Moreover, by applying
\Psi_{1}^{-1}
, Equation (
5.43
) can be written as
(\sigma_{1}^{*}\circ\sigma_{2})(X)=-\iota_{X}B
, for all
X\in TM
, thus,
\Psi_{1}^{-1}(K_{2})={\,\rm e}\,^{-B}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
and
\Psi_{1}^{-1}(K_{2}^{\perp})={\,\rm e}\,^{B}(TM)+\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
. Therefore, the condition
(i)
yields that
T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\subseteq\ker(B^{\flat})
. Note that the same condition also follows from
(ii)
.

Proposition 5.86
.

Let
(\mathbb{T}M,H)
be the twisted standard Courant algebroid isomorphic to
E
via
\Psi_{1}
, and suppose that the assumptions of Proposition
5.56
hold.
Then
B\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{2}(M)
defined in Remark
5.42
takes the form

(5.87)

\displaystyle B=B_{\rm ver}+B_{\rm hor}+B_{\rm mix}\ ,

where

(5.88)

\displaystyle B_{\rm mix}\ \in\ \mathsf{\Gamma}\bigl((T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}))\wedge(\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\cap T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\bigr)

gives an isomorphism between
T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap(\mathrm{Ann}^{*}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
and
\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\cap T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{2},
whereas

(5.89)

\displaystyle B_{\rm ver}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}\left(\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{\rm hor}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}\left(\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\right)

vanish on

(5.90)

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Gamma}\bigl((T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\wedge(T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}))\bigr)

and

(5.91)

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Gamma}\bigl((\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\cap\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}))\wedge(\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\cap T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{2})\bigr)\ ,

respectively.

Proof.

We find the conditions
B
must meet to satisfy the conditions
(i)
or
(ii)
of Theorem
5.46
.

Write
N^{*}{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}})
. We can decompose

(5.92)

\displaystyle B=B_{\rm ver}+B_{\rm hor}+B_{\rm mix}

where

(5.93)

\displaystyle B_{\rm ver}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\ ,\quad B_{\rm hor}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{2}N^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{\rm mix}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(N^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\wedge T^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{1})\ .

Recall that
\Psi_{1}^{-1}(K_{2})={\,\rm e}\,^{-B}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
.
Thus

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}(K_{2})\cap\Psi_{1}^{-1}(K_{1}^{\perp})=\set{v_{2}-\iota_{v_{2}}B\ }{\ v_{2}\in T{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\ ,\ \iota_{v_{2}}B\in\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{1})}\ .

Similarly

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}(K_{2}^{\perp})\cap\Psi_{1}^{-1}(K_{1})=\set{v_{1}\in T{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\ }{\ \iota_{v_{1}}B\in\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}}_{2})}\ .

Conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
of Theorem
5.46
state that these must be respectively contained in
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
